THE WEIYANG-GONG PALACE SITE IN HAN CHANG'AN CITY:

EXCAVATIONS IN 1980-1989

(Abstract)

Weiyang-gong was an imperial palace in the Western Han capital Chang'an City, the political , economic and cultural centre of the united, centralized empire which underwent the prosperous stage of the earlier period of ancient Chinese feudal society. As the political core of the capital, it embodied concentratedly the wish and idea of the Western Han supreme ruler, and reflected rather completely the important institutions of the Empire. Its site was archaeologicallyExplorated by extensive, systematic survey and selective excavation in the 1980s.

The Palace has a roughly square plan and measures $8800\mathrm{m}$ in perimeter and $2033 - 2190\mathrm{m}$ each side, being the largest imperial palace in ancient China. It was built at the beginning of the Western Han in the southwest of the city of Chang'an, which is $25,700\mathrm{m}$ in perimeter and somewhat square in plan, with the northern and southern sides a little irregular. On each side of the Palace there is a gate, which, as shown by trial digging, has one gateway. At the four corners of the palace city walls there must have been watching towers, of which the southwestern one has been excavated and found to have a L-shaped foundation $67.7\mathrm{m}$ from the west to the east and $31.5\mathrm{m}$ from the north to the south. Within the limits of Weiyang-gong the anterior hall complex was the most important building, of which there remains a platform-shaped foundation, being $400\mathrm{m}$ long from the north to the south and $200\mathrm{m}$ wide from the west to the east and gradually raised from south to north, with the top at the northern end measuring $15\mathrm{m}$ high. On this foundation, three ruined large-sized halls were discovered to be $3476,8280$ and $4230\mathrm{sqm}$ respectively; the middle one must be the remains of the main building of the anterior hall complex. To the west and east of the complex there stretched wingrooms for guards and attendants.

To the north of the anterior hall complex there is the site of another large-sized hall complex, which may have belonged to the empress. It comprises the major hall (in the south; $54.7\mathrm{m}$ from the west to the east), subordinate ones and other auxiliary buildings (in the north). Between the subordinate halls are lanes; this is the first time archaeologists have encountered such design in excavations of Han vestiges.

To the northwest of the anterior hall complex are the ruins of the shaofu's office (or offices under it), the middle of which consists of two large halls arranged in the north and south respectively. The southern hall is $46.1\mathrm{m}$ long from the west to the east and $12.9\mathrm{m}$ deep from the south to the north; the northern one, 31 and $12.9\mathrm{m}$ respectively. To the north of these halls is a court, $54.4\mathrm{m}$ from the west to the east and $14.7\mathrm{m}$ from the north to the south.

Auxiliary buildings are on the western and eastern sides of the large halls. In this complex, most of the rooms have wooden floors, which is the only example discovered so far in Qin-Han archaeology.

In the western part of the Weiyang-gong site is a ruined central government office, which measures $136.2\mathrm{m}$ from the west to the east and $69.4\mathrm{m}$ from the north to the south. It comprises two courts arranged side by side from west to east, leading to each other, and each having two parallel rows of houses, one in the north, the other in the south. The houses all face to the south and have a doorway each. Usually they are rather commodious, the smallest being $9.24\mathrm{sq}\mathrm{m}$ , while the largest, $212.5\mathrm{sq}\mathrm{m}$ . The 50,000 inscribed bone labels unearthed from this complex constitute an important discovery in Han archaeology.

The layout of Weiyang-gong centres on the anterior hall complex, north of which is the empress's hall complex. Farther to the north are mainly imperial cultural buildings, such as Tianlu-ge 天禄阁, Shiqu-ge 石渠阁 and Chengming-lu 承明庐。In the northwest, there is a made lake (Cang-chi Lake 沧池) covering 196,000 sq m, with an earth mound or something built within it; this design pioneered the construction of the artificial lake and mound in the palace city, prevailing in later dynasties. A number of hall-foundations were discovered on the western and eastern sides of the anterior hall complex. The artery from the northern gate to the southern one of Weiyang-gong is the palace city's axis, which extends southward beyond the Xi'an-men Gate (southern city-gate), with ancestral and divine temples built on both sides; it stretches northward and connects with Heng-men Gage Street 横门大街. This design exerted deep-going influence on the layout and shape of later palace cities.
